# Grading sheet

Grade each answer in eval/human_labels.csv (id, correct, supported, notes) before opening eval/verdicts.jsonl or eval/results.csv.

- correct = 1 if it matches the reference answer, or properly says the documents do not cover it
- supported = 1 if every claim is in the passages the assistant retrieved

## q01 (single)

**Question:** What is the maximum height I can fly my drone at in the open category?

**Reference:** Within 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth, except when overflying an obstacle as specified in Part A of the Annex.

**Answer:**

In the open category, you must maintain your unmanned aircraft within 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.010]. 

Additional rules and exceptions apply to this limit:
* When flying within a horizontal distance of 50 metres from an artificial obstacle taller than 105 metres, the maximum height may be increased up to 15 metres above the height of the obstacle at the request of the entity responsible for the obstacle [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.010].
* Unmanned sailplanes with a maximum take-off mass (MTOM), including payload, of less than 10 kg may be flown at a distance in excess of 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth, provided the sailplane is not flown at a height greater than 120 metres above the remote pilot at any time [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.010].

5 passages for 'maximum height open category UAS.OPEN.010', best match first. Cite each fact with the citation in brackets exactly as written.

1. [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.010]
   Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/947 (operation of unmanned aircraft) | Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.010, p. 24 | similarity 0.82
   ANNEX UAS OPERATIONS IN THE ‘OPEN’ AND ‘SPECIFIC’ CATEGORIES PART A UAS OPERATIONS IN THE ‘OPEN’ CATEGORY UAS.OPEN.010 General provisions (1) The category of UAS ‘open’ operations is divided into three subcategories A1, A2 and A3, on the basis of operational limitations, requirements for the remote pilot and technical requirements for UAS. (2) Where the UAS operation involves the flight of the unmanned aircraft starting from a natural elevation in the terrain or over terrain with natural elevations, the unmanned aircraft shall be maintained within 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth. The measurement of distances shall be adapted accordingly to the geographical characteristics of the terrain, such as plains, hills, mountains. (3) When flying an unmanned aircraft within a horizontal distance of 50 metres from an artificial obstacle taller than 105 metres, the maximum height of the UAS operation may be increased up to 15 metres above the height of the obstacle at the request of the entity responsible for the obstacle. (4) By way of derogation from point (2), unmanned sailplanes with a MTOM, including payload, of less than 10 kg, may be flown at a distance in excess of 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth, provided that the unmanned sailplane is not flown at a height greater than 120 metres above the remote pilot at any time.

## q02 (single)

**Question:** In subcategory A1 with a C1-class drone, can I fly over uninvolved people, and over a crowd?

**Reference:** With a C0 or C1 (or legacy/privately built sub-250 g) drone the remote pilot may overfly uninvolved persons but shall never overfly assemblies of people.

**Answer:**

In subcategory A1 with a C1-class drone:

* **Uninvolved people:** You may overfly uninvolved persons [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.020]. In the event of an unexpected overflight of uninvolved persons, you must reduce as much as possible the time during which the unmanned aircraft overflies those persons [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.020]. 
* **Assemblies of people (crowds):** You shall **never** overfly assemblies of people [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.020].

## q03 (single)

**Question:** How far do I have to stay from uninvolved people when flying in subcategory A2, and can I get closer?

**Reference:** At least 30 m horizontally and never over them; down to a minimum of 5 m with an active low-speed mode, after evaluating weather, the aircraft's performance and segregation of the overflown area.

**Answer:**

When flying in subcategory A2, you must ensure the unmanned aircraft does not overfly uninvolved persons and takes place at a safe horizontal distance of at least 30 metres from them [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030]. 

Yes, you can get closer: the remote pilot may reduce the horizontal safety distance down to a minimum of 5 metres from an uninvolved person when operating an unmanned aircraft with an active low speed mode function and after evaluating the situation regarding weather conditions, the performance of the unmanned aircraft, and the segregation of the overflown area [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].

## q04 (single)

**Question:** How far from residential or commercial areas must I stay when flying in subcategory A3?

**Reference:** At least 150 metres horizontally from residential, commercial, industrial or recreational areas.

**Answer:**

When flying in subcategory A3, you must maintain a safe horizontal distance of at least 150 metres from residential, commercial, industrial, or recreational areas [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.040]. Additionally, operations must be conducted in an area where the remote pilot reasonably expects that no uninvolved person will be endangered within the range where the unmanned aircraft is flown [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.040].

   UAS.OPEN.040 UAS operations in subcategory A3 UAS operations in subcategory A3 shall comply with all of the following conditions: (1) be conducted in an area where the remote pilot reasonably expects that no uninvolved person will be endangered within the range where the unmanned aircraft is flown during the entire time of the UAS operation; (2) be conducted at a safe horizontal distance of at least 150 metres from residential, commercial, industrial or recreational areas; (3) be performed by a remote pilot who is familiar with manufacturer’s instructions provided by the manufacturer of the UAS and who has completed an online training course and passed an online theoretical knowledge examination as defined in point (4)(b) of point UAS.OPEN.020; (4) be performed with an unmanned aircraft that: (a) has an MTOM, including payload, of less than 25 kg, in the case of a privately built UAS, or (b) meets the requirements defined in point (b) of Article 20; (c) is marked as class C2 and complies with the requirements of that class, as defined in Part 3 of the Annex to Delegated Regulation (EU) 2019/945 and is operated with active and updated direct remote identification system and geo-awareness function or; (d) is marked as class C3 and complies with the requirements of that class, as defined in Part 4 of the Annex to Delegated Regulation (EU) 2019/945 and is operated with active and updated direct remote identification system and geo-awareness function; or (e) is marked as class C4 and complies with the requirements of that class, as defined in Part 5 of the Annex to Delegated Regulation (EU) 2019/945.

   ANNEX UAS OPERATIONS IN THE ‘OPEN’ AND ‘SPECIFIC’ CATEGORIES PART A UAS OPERATIONS IN THE ‘OPEN’ CATEGORY UAS.OPEN.010 General provisions (1) The category of UAS ‘open’ operations is divided into three subcategories A1, A2 and A3, on the basis of operational limitations, requirements for the remote pilot and technical requirements for UAS. (2) Where the UAS operation involves the flight of the unmanned aircraft starting from a natural elevation in the terrain or over terrain with natural elevations, the unmanned aircraft shall be maintained within 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth. The measurement of distances shall be adapted accordingly to the geographical characteristics of the terrain, such as plains, hills, mountains. (3) When flying an unmanned aircraft within a horizontal distance of 50 metres from an artificial obstacle taller than 105 metres, the maximum height of the UAS operation may be increased up to 15 metres above the height of the obstacle at the request of the entity responsible for the obstacle. (4) By way of derogation from point (2), unmanned sailplanes with a MTOM, including payload, of less than 10 kg, may be flown at a distance in excess of 120 metres from the closest point of the surface of the earth, provided that the unmanned sailplane is not flown at a height greater than 120 metres above the remote pilot at any time.

## q05 (single)

**Question:** What steps do I need to complete to get the A2 certificate of remote pilot competency?

**Reference:** In order: the online training course and online exam (A1/A3); self-practical training in A3 conditions; declaring that training and passing an additional theory exam (at least 30 multiple-choice questions, 75% to pass) with the competent authority or a designated entity, on meteorology, UAS flight performance and ground-risk mitigations.

**Answer:**

To obtain the A2 certificate of remote pilot competency, you must comply with the following conditions in the order indicated:

1. Complete an online training course and pass the online theoretical knowledge examination [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].
2. Complete a self-practical training in the operating conditions of subcategory A3 [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].
3. Declare the completion of the self-practical training and pass an additional theoretical knowledge examination provided by the competent authority or a designated entity, achieving at least 75% of the overall marks [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].

## q07 (single)

**Question:** What is the minimum age to be a remote pilot in the open category?

**Reference:** 16 years. No minimum age for a C0 toy in A1, privately built drones under 250 g, or flying under direct supervision; Member States may lower it by up to 4 years for the open category.

**Answer:**

The minimum age for remote pilots operating a UAS in the open category is 16 years [reg-2019-947, Article 9]. 

However, no minimum age is required when:
* Operating in subcategory A1 with a class C0 UAS that is a toy under Directive 2009/48/EC [reg-2019-947, Article 9].
* Operating a privately-built UAS with a maximum take-off mass of less than 250 g [reg-2019-947, Article 9].
* Operating under the direct supervision of a remote pilot who meets the age and competency requirements [reg-2019-947, Article 9].

Additionally, Member States may lower the minimum age for open-category operations by up to 4 years, though those pilots will only be allowed to operate on that Member State's territory [reg-2019-947, Article 9].

## q08 (single)

**Question:** How long is my A2 certificate of remote pilot competency valid?

**Reference:** Five years; it can be revalidated within that period.

**Answer:**

Your A2 certificate of remote pilot competency is valid for five years [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.070]. 

Within its validity period, it can be revalidated by demonstrating the required competencies or by completing a refresher training provided by the competent authority or an entity designated by it [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.070]. If the certificate expires, you must revalidate it by demonstrating the required competencies [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.070].

## q09 (single)

**Question:** What counts as an 'assembly of people' under the EU drone rules?

**Reference:** Gatherings where persons are unable to move away due to the density of the people present.

**Answer:**

Under EU drone rules, 'assemblies of people' are defined as gatherings where persons are unable to move away due to the density of the people present [reg-2019-947, Article 2].

## q11 (single)

**Question:** I sometimes shoot after sunset. What must I do when flying in the open category at night?

**Reference:** Ensure that a green flashing light on the unmanned aircraft is activated.

**Answer:**

When operating at night, you must ensure that a green flashing light on the unmanned aircraft is activated [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.060].

## q12 (single)

**Question:** Before an A2 or A3 shoot with my client's staff on site, what must I make sure of regarding the people involved?

**Reference:** That all involved persons present in the area of the operation have been informed of the risks and have explicitly agreed to participate.

**Answer:**

For an A2 or A3 shoot involving your client's staff, you must ensure that all staff members present are either participating in the operation or are fully aware of the instructions and safety precautions given by the UAS operator, so they do not count as uninvolved persons [reg-2019-947, Article 2]. 

Additionally, depending on the subcategory:
* **Subcategory A2:** You must conduct the operation so that the unmanned aircraft does not overfly uninvolved persons and maintains a safe horizontal distance of at least 30 metres from them (which may be reduced down to a minimum of 5 metres when using an active low speed mode function after evaluating weather, performance, and segregation) [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].
* **Subcategory A3:** You must conduct the operation in an area where you can reasonably expect that no uninvolved person will be endangered within the range where the aircraft is flown during the entire operation [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.040].

## q13 (single)

**Question:** My drone has no class label. Can I keep flying it in the open category, and under which conditions?

**Reference:** Yes if it was placed on the market before 1 January 2024 and is not privately built: in A1 if its MTOM is under 250 g, or in A3 if its MTOM is under 25 kg.

**Answer:**

Yes, you can keep flying a drone without a class label in the open category if it was placed on the market before 1 January 2024 and is not privately built [reg-2019-947, Article 20]. 

Depending on your drone's maximum take-off mass (including payload/fuel), it must be operated under the following conditions:
* **In subcategory A1**, provided that the unmanned aircraft has a maximum take-off mass of less than 250 g [reg-2019-947, Article 20].
* **In subcategory A3**, provided that the unmanned aircraft has a maximum take-off mass of less than 25 kg [reg-2019-947, Article 20].

   Whereas: (1) The unmanned aircraft systems (‘UAS’) whose operation presents the lowest risks and that belong to the ‘open’ category of operations should not be subject to classic aeronautical compliance procedures. The possibility to establish Community harmonisation legislation as referred to in paragraph 6 of Article 56 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139 should be used for those UAS. Consequently, it is necessary to set out the requirements that address the risks posed by the operation of those UAS, taking full account of other applicable Union harmonisation legislation. (2) These requirements should cover the essential requirements provided for in Article 55 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139, in particular as regards the specific features and functionalities necessary to mitigate risks pertaining to the safety of the flight, privacy, and protection of personal data, security or the environment, arising from the operation of these UAS. (3) When manufacturers place a UAS on the market with the intention to make it available for operations under the ‘open’ category and therefore affix a class identification label on it, they should ensure compliance of the UAS with the requirements of that class. (4) Considering the good level of safety achieved by model aircraft already made available on the market, it is appropriate to create the C4 class of UAS which should not be subject to disproportionate technical requirements for the benefit of model aircraft operators. (5) This Regulation should also apply to UAS, which are considered as toys within the meaning of Directive 2009/48/EC of the European Parliament

## q14 (single)

**Question:** What is the weight limit for a class C1 drone?

**Reference:** MTOM of less than 900 g including payload, or alternatively designed so that impact energy to a human head is less than 80 J; maximum speed in level flight 19 m/s.

**Answer:**

A class C1 unmanned aircraft system must have a maximum take-off mass (MTOM) of less than 900 g, including payload, or alternatively be made of materials and have performance and physical characteristics such as to ensure that in the event of an impact at terminal velocity with a human head, the energy transmitted to the human head is less than 80 J [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].

   PART 2 Requirements for a class C1 Unmanned aircraft system A class C1 UAS bears the following class identification label on the UA: A class C1 UAS shall comply with the following: (1) be made of materials and have performance and physical characteristics such as to ensure that in the event of an impact at terminal velocity with a human head, the energy transmitted to the human head is less than 80 J, or, as an alternative, shall have an MTOM of less than 900 g, including payload; (2) have a maximum speed in level flight of 19 m/s; (3) have a maximum attainable height above the take-off point limited to 120 m or be equipped with a system that limits the height above the surface or above the take-off point to 120 m or to a value selectable by the remote pilot; if the value is selectable, clear information about the height of the UA above the surface or take-off point during flight shall be provided to the remote pilot; (4) be safely controllable with regards to stability, manoeuvrability and the command and control link performance, by a remote pilot with adequate competency as defined in Implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/947 and following the manufacturer’s instructions, as necessary under all anticipated operating conditions including following the failure of one or, if appropriate, more systems; (5) have the requisite mechanical strength for the UA, including any necessary safety factor, and, where appropriate, stability to withstand any stress to which it is

## q15 (single)

**Question:** What speed is a C2 drone limited to in its low-speed mode?

**Reference:** A ground speed of no more than 3 m/s (required unless it is a fixed-wing UA).

**Answer:**

Unless it is a fixed-wing unmanned aircraft, a class C2 drone must be equipped with a low-speed mode selectable by the remote pilot that limits its ground speed to no more than 3 m/s [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 3].

## q16 (single)

**Question:** What maximum mass and size can a class C3 drone have?

**Reference:** MTOM of less than 25 kg including payload and a maximum characteristic dimension of less than 3 m.

**Answer:**

A class C3 drone must have a maximum take-off mass (MTOM) of less than 25 kg, including payload, and a maximum characteristic dimension of less than 3 m [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 4].

## q17 (single)

**Question:** What information does the direct remote identification of a C1 drone broadcast during the flight?

**Reference:** At least: the operator registration number and verification code, the UA's unique serial number, a time-stamp with the UA's position and height, its route course and ground speed, the remote pilot's position (or take-off point), and the emergency status.

**Answer:**

During the flight, the direct remote identification of a C1 drone ensures the direct periodic broadcast of at least the following data:

* The UAS operator registration number and the verification code provided by the Member State of registration during the registration process (unless the consistency check fails) [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].
* The unique physical serial number of the unmanned aircraft [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].
* The time stamp, the geographical position of the unmanned aircraft, and its height above the surface or take-off point [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].
* The route course measured clockwise from true north and the ground speed of the unmanned aircraft [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].
* The geographical position of the remote pilot or, if not available, the take-off point [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 2].
* An indication of the emergency status of the UAS [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 3].

<details><summary>Retrieved passages</summary>

```

   PART 6 Requirements for a direct remote identification add-on A direct remote identification add-on shall comply with the following: (1) allow the upload of the UAS operator registration number required in accordance with Article 14 of Implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/947 and any additional number provided by the registration system; the system shall perform a consistency check verifying the integrity of the full string provided to the UAS operator at the time of registration; in case of inconsistency, the system shall emit an error message to the UAS operator;    UAS.SPEC.060 Responsibilities of the remote pilot (1) The remote pilot shall: (a) not perform duties under the influence of psychoactive substances or alcohol or when it is unfit to perform its tasks due to injury, fatigue, medication, sickness or other causes; (b) have the appropriate remote pilot competency as defined in the operational authorisation, in the standard scenario defined in Appendix 1 or as defined by the LUC and carry a proof of competency while operating the UAS. (c) be familiar with manufacturer’s instructions provided by the manufacturer of the UAS. (3) During the flight, the remote pilot shall: (a) comply with the authorised or declared limitations and conditions; (b) avoid any risk of collision with any manned aircraft and discontinue a flight when continuing it may pose a risk to other aircraft, people, animals, environment or property; (c) comply with the operational limitations in geographical zones defined in accordance with Article 15; (d) comply with the operator’s procedures; (e) not fly close to or inside areas where an emergency response effort is ongoing unless they have permission to do so from the responsible emergency response services.

## q18 (single)

**Question:** Under the STS-01 standard scenario, what ground speed limit applies and which drone class must I use?

**Reference:** Ground speed below 5 m/s for untethered aircraft, flown in VLOS over a controlled ground area, with an unmanned aircraft marked as class C5.

**Answer:**

Under the STS-01 standard scenario, untethered unmanned aircraft must be conducted at a ground speed of less than 5 m/s `[reg-2019-947, Annex > Appendix 1 > Chapter I > UAS.STS-01.020]`. 

The operation must be conducted with an unmanned aircraft which is marked as class C5 and complies with the requirements of that class, as defined in Part 16 of the Annex to Delegated Regulation (EU) 2019/945 `[reg-2019-947, Annex > Appendix 1 > Chapter I > UAS.STS-01.020]`.

## q19 (single)

**Question:** Can a Member State such as Spain restrict or ban drone flights in certain zones? What can it require there?

**Reference:** Yes, in UAS geographical zones it may prohibit some or all operations, impose conditions or require prior flight authorisation, impose environmental standards, and allow only certain classes or UAS with remote identification or geo-awareness.

**Answer:**

Yes, Member States may prohibit certain or all UAS operations, request particular conditions, or require a prior flight authorisation for certain or all UAS operations when defining UAS geographical zones for safety, security, privacy, or environmental reasons [reg-2019-947, Article 15]. 

In these zones, Member States may also:
* Subject UAS operations to specified environmental standards [reg-2019-947, Article 15].
* Allow access only to certain UAS classes [reg-2019-947, Article 15].
* Allow access only to UAS equipped with certain technical features, such as remote identification systems or geo-awareness systems [reg-2019-947, Article 15].
* Designate zones where operations are exempt from one or more 'open' category requirements, based on a risk assessment [reg-2019-947, Article 15].

## q20 (single)

**Question:** My planned shoot does not fit the open category. What do I need to do?

**Reference:** Obtain an operational authorisation (Article 12) from the competent authority of the Member State of registration, submitting a risk assessment with mitigations; or, for an operation matching a standard scenario, submit a declaration instead.

**Answer:**

Where one of the requirements for the open category is not met, a UAS operator is required to obtain an operational authorisation from the competent authority in the Member State where it is registered [reg-2019-947, Article 5]. To apply, the operator must perform a risk assessment in accordance with Article 11 and submit it together with the application, including adequate mitigating measures [reg-2019-947, Article 5].

Alternatively, the UAS operator may submit an operational declaration of compliance with a standard scenario to the competent authority of the Member State of registration [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part B > UAS.SPEC.020]. An operational authorisation or declaration is not required if the UAS operator holds a light UAS operator certificate (LUC) with appropriate privileges, or for operations conducted in the framework of model aircraft clubs and associations that have received an authorisation in accordance with Article 16 [reg-2019-947, Article 5].

## q21 (single)

**Question:** Which U-space services are mandatory for every flight in U-space airspace?

**Reference:** Network identification, geo-awareness, UAS flight authorisation and traffic information services.

**Answer:**

All UAS operations in U-space airspace are subject to the following mandatory U-space services:
(a) the network identification service;
(b) the geo-awareness service;
(c) the UAS flight authorisation service;
(d) the traffic information service.

[reg-2021-664, Chapter II > Article 3]

## q22 (single)

**Question:** Does the U-space regulation apply to me if I fly a C0 drone in subcategory A1?

**Reference:** No. It does not apply to A1 operations with a class C0 drone (or a privately built drone under 250 g and under 19 m/s).

**Answer:**

No, the U-space regulation does not apply to you when flying a class C0 drone in subcategory A1 [reg-2021-664, Chapter I > Article 1]. 

Specifically, the U-space regulation does not apply to operations of UAS conducted in subcategory A1 of the 'open' category with an unmanned aircraft that is marked as class C0 and complies with the requirements of that class [reg-2021-664, Chapter I > Article 1].

   Article 1 Subject matter and scope 1. This Regulation lays down rules and procedures for the safe operations of UAS in the U-space airspace, for the safe integration of UAS into the aviation system and for the provision of U-space services. 2. This Regulation shall apply, within the UAS geographical zones established as U-space airspace by Member States, to: (a) operators of UAS; (b) U-space service providers; (c) providers of common information services. 3. ## q23 (single)

**Question:** Before each flight in U-space airspace, what do I have to submit, and to whom?

**Reference:** A UAS flight authorisation request to its U-space service provider, through the UAS flight authorisation service, in line with Annex IV.

**Answer:**

Before each individual flight in a U-space airspace, the UAS operator must submit a UAS flight authorisation request to its U-space service provider, through the UAS flight authorisation service, in compliance with Annex IV [reg-2021-664, Chapter III > Article 6].

The request must comprise the following information:
1. The unique serial number of the unmanned aircraft or, if the unmanned aircraft is privately built, the unique serial number of the add-on [reg-2021-664, Annex IV].
2. Mode of operation [reg-2021-664, Annex IV].
3. Type of flight (special operations) [reg-2021-664, Annex IV].

## q24 (single)

**Question:** Why does EU law require registration for drone operators flying aircraft of 250 g or more?

**Reference:** Because studies demonstrated that unmanned aircraft with a take-off mass of 250 g or more present risks to security (recital 15).

**Answer:**

EU law requires UAS operators to register themselves when operating unmanned aircraft with a take-off mass of 250 g or more in the 'open' category because studies have demonstrated that such aircraft present risks to security [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals].

## q25 (single)

**Question:** Which kinds of places does the EU consider particularly sensitive to drone operations?

**Reference:** Hospitals, gatherings of people, installations such as penal institutions or industrial plants, top-level and higher-level government authorities, nature conservation areas and certain transport infrastructure (recital 21).

**Answer:**

The EU considers the following types of places particularly sensitive to some or all types of unmanned aircraft system (UAS) operations:
* Hospitals [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]
* Gatherings of people [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]
* Installations and facilities such as penal institutions or industrial plants [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]
* Top-level and higher-level government authorities [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]
* Nature conservation areas [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]
* Certain items of transport infrastructure [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals]

## q26 (multi)

**Question:** I fly a 249 g C0 drone with a camera. Do I need to register as an operator, and what is the C0 weight limit?

**Reference:** Yes, operators must register when the aircraft has a sensor able to capture personal data, unless it is a toy under Directive 2009/48/EC (Art. 14(5)); a C0 must have an MTOM of less than 250 g including payload (Part 1).

**Answer:**

You do not need to register as a UAS operator if you operate within the "open" category and the unmanned aircraft has a Maximum Take-Off Mass (MTOM) of less than 250 g and is not equipped with a sensor able to capture personal data [reg-2019-947, Article 14]. However, because your C0 drone is equipped with a camera (which is a sensor able to capture personal data), you are required to register as a UAS operator [reg-2019-947, Article 14].

A class C0 unmanned aircraft system must have an MTOM of less than 250 g, including payload [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 1].

## q27 (multi)

**Question:** With a C2 drone in A2 using low-speed mode, how close can I get to uninvolved people, and how fast can the drone go in that mode?

**Reference:** Down to 5 m horizontally (instead of 30 m) after evaluating the situation (UAS.OPEN.030); the low-speed mode limits ground speed to no more than 3 m/s (Part 3).

**Answer:**

When operating a C2 drone in subcategory A2 with an active low-speed mode, you can reduce the horizontal safety distance down to a minimum of **5 metres** from an uninvolved person, after evaluating weather conditions, drone performance, and the segregation of the overflown area [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030]. Without low-speed mode, operations must maintain a safe horizontal distance of at least 30 metres and not overfly uninvolved persons [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030].

In that low-speed mode, if the unmanned aircraft is not a fixed-wing UA, the mode must limit the ground speed to no more than **3 m/s** [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 3].

## q28 (multi)

**Question:** Which open subcategory lets me fly a C3-class drone, and what are the C3 mass and size limits?

**Reference:** Subcategory A3, at least 150 m from residential, commercial, industrial or recreational areas (UAS.OPEN.040); C3 MTOM under 25 kg and characteristic dimension under 3 m (Part 4).

**Answer:**

A C3-class drone is operated in **subcategory A3** `[reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.040]`.

The mass and size limits for a class C3 unmanned aircraft system are:
* **Maximum Take-Off Mass (MTOM):** Less than 25 kg, including payload `[reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 4]`.
* **Size limit:** A maximum characteristic dimension of less than 3 m `[reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 4]`.

## q29 (multi)

**Question:** STS-01 requires a C5 drone. What low-speed mode must a C5 drone have, and what ground speed limit applies in STS-01?

**Reference:** A C5 must have a pilot-selectable low-speed mode limiting ground speed to no more than 5 m/s unless tethered (Part 16); STS-01 flights must be below 5 m/s ground speed for untethered aircraft (UAS.STS-01.020).

**Answer:**

Unless tethered, a class C5 drone must be equipped with a low-speed mode selectable by the remote pilot that limits the ground speed to not more than 5 m/s [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 16]. 

For operations in STS-01, untethered unmanned aircraft must be conducted at a ground speed of less than 5 m/s [reg-2019-947, Annex > Appendix 1 > Chapter I > UAS.STS-01.020].

## q30 (multi)

**Question:** Why do operators of camera drones have to register, and in which country do I register?

**Reference:** Because of the risks to privacy and protection of personal data (recital 16); in the Member State of residence (natural persons) or principal place of business (legal persons), and in only one Member State at a time (Art. 14(6)).

**Answer:**

Operators of camera drones must register because they operate unmanned aircraft equipped with a sensor able to capture personal data (unless the drone is considered a toy within the meaning of Directive 2009/48/EC), or because they operate in the open category with a maximum take-off mass (MTOM) of 250 g or more (or an aircraft that can transfer above 80 Joules of kinetic energy to a human upon impact), or because they operate any drone in the specific category [reg-2019-947-recitals, Recitals], [reg-2019-947, Article 14].

You must register yourself in the Member State where you have your residence (for natural persons) or where you have your principal place of business (for legal persons) [reg-2019-947, Article 14].

## q31 (multi)

**Question:** Is there a minimum age to fly a C0 toy drone in A1, and what mass limit must a C0 drone respect?

**Reference:** No minimum age applies in A1 with a C0 that is a toy under Directive 2009/48/EC (Art. 9(2)(a)); a C0 must have an MTOM of less than 250 g (Part 1).

**Answer:**

There is no minimum age for remote pilots operating a class C0 UAS that is a toy within the meaning of Directive 2009/48/EC in subcategory A1 [reg-2019-947, Article 9]. 

A class C0 drone must have a maximum take-off mass (MTOM) of less than 250 g, including payload [reg-2019-945, Annex > Part 1].

## q32 (unanswerable)

**Question:** Do I have to notify the Spanish Ministry of the Interior before flying over a street in Madrid?

**Reference:** Say the documents do not cover Spanish national notification rules; at most mention that Member States may set conditions in geographical zones (Art. 15), without inventing a Spanish requirement.

**Answer:**

The documents do not cover this. 

Member States may define UAS geographical zones for safety, security, privacy, or environmental reasons, which can include prohibiting operations, requesting particular conditions, or requiring prior flight authorisation `[reg-2019-947, Article 15]`.

(15) Studies have demonstrated that unmanned aircraft with a take-off mass of 250 g or more would present risks to security and therefore UAS operators of such unmanned aircraft should be required to register themselves when operating such aircraft in the ‘open’ category. (7) Ensure in the case of an UAS operation in subcategory A2 or A3, that all involved persons present in the area of the operation have been informed of the risks and have explicitly agreed to participate.

## q34 (unanswerable)

**Question:** What minimum third-party liability insurance cover do I need for a 900 g drone?

**Reference:** Say the documents give no minimum insurance amount; they only mention an insurance policy number if required by Union or national law.

**Answer:**

The documents do not cover this. 

The EU regulations mention that UAS operator registration systems must provide a field for "an insurance policy number for UAS if required by Union or national law" [reg-2019-947, Article 14], but they do not specify a minimum third-party liability insurance cover requirement or amount.

## q35 (unanswerable)

**Question:** How much does AESA charge for the A2 certificate exam?

**Reference:** Say the documents do not state any exam fees.

**Answer:**

The documents do not cover this. The regulations state that the additional theoretical knowledge examination for subcategory A2 is provided by the competent authority or at an entity designated by the competent authority of a Member State [reg-2019-947, Annex > Part A > UAS.OPEN.030], but they do not specify any examination fees or costs charged by AESA.

## q36 (unanswerable)

**Question:** Is the centre of Madrid designated as U-space airspace?

**Reference:** Say the documents do not say which areas are designated as U-space airspace; Member States designate it.

**Answer:**

The documents do not cover this. The EU regulations establish the rules for how Member States may designate U-space airspace [reg-2021-664, Chapter II > Article 3], but they do not specify the individual geographical locations or cities, such as Madrid, where U-space airspace has been designated.

<details><summary>Retrieved passages</summary>

```
5 passages for 'Madrid U-space airspace designated', best match first. Cite each fact with the citation in brackets exactly as written.

1. [reg-2021-664, Chapter II > Article 3]
   Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2021/664 (U-space regulatory framework) | Chapter II > Article 3, pp. 3-4 | similarity 0.72
   CHAPTER II U-SPACE AIRSPACE AND COMMON INFORMATION SERVICES Article 3 U-space airspace 1. Where Member States designate U-space airspace for safety, security, privacy or environmental reasons such designation shall be supported by an airspace risk assessment. 2. All UAS operations in the U-space airspace shall be subject to at least the following mandatory U-space services: (a) the network identification service referred to in Article 8; (b) the geo-awareness service referred to in Article 9; (c) the UAS flight authorisation service referred to in Article 10; (d) the traffic information service referred to in Article 11. 3. For each U-space airspace, based on the airspace risk assessment referred to in paragraph 1, Member States may require additional U-space services selected from the services referred to in Articles 12 and 13. 4. For each U-space airspace, based on the airspace risk assessment referred to in paragraph 1 and using the criteria set out in Annex I, Member States shall determine: (a) the UAS capabilities and performance requirements; (b) the U-space services performance requirements; (c) the applicable operational conditions and airspace constraints. 5. Member States shall give access to U-space service providers to the relevant data, if required for the application of this Regulation, as regards to: (a) the UAS operators registration system, referred to in Article 14 of Implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/947, of the Member State in which U-space service providers offer their services; and (b) UAS operators registration systems of other Member States through the repository of information referred to in Article 74 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139. 6. Member States shall make the information on the U-space airspace available in accordance with Article 15(3) of Regulation (EU) 2019/947, as well as through their aeronautical information service. 7. Where Member States decide to establish a cross-border U-space airspace, they shall jointly decide on: (a) the designation of the cross-border U-space airspace; (b) the provision of cross-border U-space services; (c) the provision of cross-border common information services.

The following definitions also apply: (1) ‘U-space airspace’ means a UAS geographical zone designated by Member States, where UAS operations are only allowed to take place with the support of U-space services; (2) ‘U-space service’ means a service relying on digital services and automation of functions designed to support safe, secure and efficient access to U-space airspace for a large number of UAS; (3) ‘airspace risk assessment’ means an evaluation of operational, safety and security risks that takes into account the required levels of safety performance as defined in the European Plan for Aviation Safety and the State Safety Programme referred to in Articles 6 and 7 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139, the type, complexity and density of the traffic, the location, altitudes or heights and the airspace classification; (4) ‘common information service’ means a service consisting in the dissemination of static and dynamic data to enable the provision of U-space services for the management of traffic of unmanned aircraft; (5) ‘principal place of business’ means the head office or registered office of a U-space or common information service provider in the Member State within which the principal financial functions and operational control of the service provider are exercised; (6) ‘dynamic airspace reconfiguration’ means the temporary modification of the U-space airspace in order to accommodate short-term changes in manned traffic demand, by adjusting the geographical limits of that U-space airspace.

   Article 1 Subject matter and scope 1. This Regulation lays down rules and procedures for the safe operations of UAS in the U-space airspace, for the safe integration of UAS into the aviation system and for the provision of U-space services. 2. This Regulation shall apply, within the UAS geographical zones established as U-space airspace by Member States, to: (a) operators of UAS; (b) U-space service providers; (c) providers of common information services. 3. This Regulation shall not apply to operations of UAS conducted: (a) in the framework of model aircraft clubs and associations that have received an authorisation in accordance with Article 16 of Implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/947; (b) in subcategory A1 of the ‘open’ category of operations with an unmanned aircraft that: (i) in the case of a privately built UAS, has a maximum take-off mass, including payload, of less than 250 g and a maximum operating speed of less than 19 m/s; or (ii) is marked as class C0 and complies with the requirements of that class, as defined in Part 1 of the Annex to Delegated Regulation (EU) 2019/945; or (c) in accordance with SERA.5015 instrument flight rules of Implementing Regulation (EU) No 923/2012.

U-space service providers shall dispatch the geo-awareness information in a timely manner to allow contingencies and emergencies to be addressed by UAS operators, and shall include its time of update together with a version number or a valid time, or both.

   Whereas: (1) The unmanned aircraft systems (‘UAS’) whose operation presents the lowest risks and that belong to the ‘open’ category of operations should not be subject to classic aeronautical compliance procedures. The possibility to establish Community harmonisation legislation as referred to in paragraph 6 of Article 56 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139 should be used for those UAS. Consequently, it is necessary to set out the requirements that address the risks posed by the operation of those UAS, taking full account of other applicable Union harmonisation legislation. (2) These requirements should cover the essential requirements provided for in Article 55 of Regulation (EU) 2018/1139, in particular as regards the specific features and functionalities necessary to mitigate risks pertaining to the safety of the flight, privacy, and protection of personal data, security or the environment, arising from the operation of these UAS. (3) When manufacturers place a UAS on the market with the intention to make it available for operations under the ‘open’ category and therefore affix a class identification label on it, they should ensure compliance of the UAS with the requirements of that class. (4) Considering the good level of safety achieved by model aircraft already made available on the market, it is appropriate to create the C4 class of UAS which should not be subject to disproportionate technical requirements for the benefit of model aircraft operators. (5) This Regulation should also apply to UAS, which are considered as toys within the meaning of Directive 2009/48/EC of the European Parliament
